    @Karl_SchamottaIt's hardly a new observation, but with foreign private sector capital flows into the US absolutely bodying the official sector*, the "reserve currency" conception of the dollar is increasingly inappropriate. It's a "portfolio" or "funding" currency now, and has been for a while:
    Two small additional observations -- 1) I would describe the dollar more as the destination currency in most carry trades than the funding currency (a shift from 07/08) 2) foreign official flows exceed reserve flows significantly, but SWF/ sovereign pension flows are often closer to private flows than old reserve flows into Treasuriespost ↗原帖 ↗

  • 05:32 · @HankInBeijing
    Aggressive indeed. Considering 5 yr plan indicators carry a mandatory completion rate requirement of 92% (one of those internal red lines), this looks even more aggressive. But nonetheless, 5 yr plans could be revised and amended as they are implemented.post ↗原帖 ↗
    @FengmingLuPEA few things to note from this five year plan for Chinese NEVs: 1. The auto roadmap last year have already set a lot of targets for 2040. This one has set more specific mid-term goals for 2030. Some are quite ambitious. 2. 3.3L/100km (or 71 mpg for US readers) for all ICEVs (mostly being HEVs and PHEVs) and 11.5kwh/100km (or 185 wh per mile) for all BEVs are pretty aggressive. The latter is way below the current Tesla Model 3 level. More tech advancements in thermal management, electric drivetrain, low wind and rolling resistance, and lightweighting are needed. 3. More conservative and much more vague goals about autonomous driving or L3/L4 ADAS. No clear timeline for L3 or L4 rollout is set yet. Clearly some concerns. But there are goals about autonomous driving on (intercity and urban) expressways and "some" urban roads, reflecting path dependency on the Chinese style Navigate on Autopilot (NOA) system. 4. Some objectives are still related to the Vehicle-Road-Cloud Integration (similar to V2X in the US), which has not picked up much traction in the last few years. But there are additional political incentives in China as there are strong lobbying groups among academics and it involves massive infrastructural spending. 5. 70% penetration for new NEV passenger vehicles by 2030 is a little bit ambitious but reachable. 40% penetration for new NEV commercial vehicles may be reached way sooner than 2030. 6. Clearer signals about promoting NEV heavy-duty trucks, mainly semi-trucks (including electric, hydrogen, hybrid, and methanol trucks). ICEV heavy-duty trucks including semi-trucks are main contributors of air pollution and carbon emission.

  • 23:15 · @kyleichan Kyle Chan
    China increasingly sees EVs and autonomous vehicles as merging into a single tech-industrial stack, as captured by the term 智能网联新能源汽车 "intelligent connected new energy vehicles." If you think EVs are "just" about climate, you're missing the bigger picture.post ↗原帖 ↗
    @leixing77BREAKING: China's MIIT (Ministry of Industry & Information Technology) and eight other ministries and commissions just released the 15th Five-Year Plan For the Industrial Development of Intelligent Connected New Energy Vehicles. The Plan sets out China’s development targets for the ICNEV industry structured as “1+4” toward 2030. The overarching goal is to further consolidate China’s advantages across the full ICNEV value chain and move into the ranks of the world’s leading automotive powers. By 2030, NEVs are expected to account for 70% of new passenger vehicle sales and 40% of new commercial vehicle sales, while vehicles with automated driving capabilities are to achieve large-scale deployment. The four supporting targets are to: 1) Improve key technologies, including reducing average passenger vehicle fuel consumption to about 3.3 L/100 km and average BEV energy consumption to about 11.5 kWh/100 km, while enabling highly automated driving on expressways, urban expressways and selected urban roads; 2) Optimize the industry structure, with labor productivity rising 15% from 2025 levels and several Chinese automakers entering the global top 10 alongside more Chinese suppliers in the global top 100; 3) Advance internationalization by strengthening overseas operating capabilities, improving the reputation of Chinese auto brands and increasing China’s influence over international standards and regulations; 4) Enhance economic and social benefits by reinforcing the auto industry’s role as a pillar of the national economy, achieving an earlier carbon peak, and accelerating the green and low-carbon transition of the supply chain. I feel like some of these targets have already been achieved or are within reach. #ChinaSpeed

  • 22:12 · @pstAsiatech Paul Triolothread · 3 posts推文串 · 3 条
    A spokesperson for the Ministry of Commerce answered reporters' questions regarding a senior CIA official's public statement about conducting espionage activities against China. https://www.mofcom.gov.cn/xwfb/xwfyrth/art/2026/art_09c2bc98f6994d509fea184c4cbf603c.htmlpost ↗原帖 ↗
    The US actions are a classic example of saying one thing and doing another. On the one hand, in 2014, the US issued Presidential Policy Directive 28, which explicitly prohibited the collection of foreign private business information or trade secrets through intelligence activities for the purpose of helping US companies or businesses gain a competitive advantage. In 2022, the US issued Executive Order 14086, reaffirming these restrictions. However, the US ignores its own self-defined boundaries, and its intelligence agencies conduct large-scale cyber espionage and intelligence theft against countries around the world, making it the world's number one habitual spy.post ↗原帖 ↗
    Now, it brazenly claims that anything constituting economic competition can be considered a legitimate target for espionage, a blatant violation of international trade rules and intellectual property protection principles. On the other hand, the US has long accused other trading partners of harming US interests through intelligence theft, yet now it characterizes its own espionage activities as legitimate and justifiable, a clear example of double standards.post ↗原帖 ↗

    @BarackObamaIf you were young in 2001, or weren’t even born yet, it can be hard to imagine what 9/11 felt like. The shock of that beautiful, cloudless morning. The heartbreak of the families whose loved ones were taken with such swiftness and cruelty. The realization that the forces of malevolence and violence were closer, and the future less certain, than we knew. But in the days that followed, we felt something else, too. Pride in the rescue workers who had rushed to the scene, and the passengers who had stormed the cockpit. Admiration for the men and women who left behind lives of comfort to serve their country. A deep resolve that nothing would break the will of a truly United States of America. Today Michelle and I are remembering those we lost that day, and those who made the ultimate sacrifice in the wars that followed. We pray for their families. And we hope Americans everywhere remember the true lesson of 9/11: that we can survive even our darkest days as long as we move forward as one nation and one people.
  • 20:40 · @niubi Bill Bishop 利明璋
    Takshashila - China is Armed for an Age of Protracted Economic Warfare - "China’s weaponisation of dependencies is not solely a response to “containment” by the US, but also reflective of its intrinsic character. The end goal of China’s tools of economic coercion isn’t limited to mere compellence, but also extends to capability denial. Whether supply chains concentrate or diffuse will depend on the ability of afflicted states to sustain a horizontal alignment among themselves and a vertical alignment with their business houses for long enough to build viable alternatives. While sustained pressure by Beijing gives the coalition a reason to hold together, selective relief on the other hand, granted to some actors and withheld from others, creates the conditions for defection, thereby thwarting the whole de-risking process."post ↗原帖 ↗

  • 18:40 · @Brad_Setser Brad Setser
    Argument also now applies to Germanypost ↗原帖 ↗
    @EvanFeigenbaumBlast from the past from me, writing 15 years ago in 2011 together with @ianbremmer in @HarvardBiz. Called it. “Conventional wisdom says that [in] U.S.-China relations, commerce plays a stabilizing role ... But in the next few years, commercial ties between the two countries will almost certainly become more competitive and could even disrupt the relationship. That’s because China wants to become a leader in just the kinds of technology sectors that have traditionally given the U.S. a global edge. The result will be increased competition between the two countries—within China and globally—and a deepening unease on both sides. China has made no secret of its goals ... China is set on developing higher-value-added and technology-intensive industries. And its huge capacity to mobilize domestic capital gives it the power to do so. For many U.S. firms, the first battleground will be within China, where there is already significant competition between foreign and local companies for market share ... And it’s a sentiment that extends beyond technology companies ... into the manufacturing sector. But the next battleground will be the global marketplace for sophisticated technologies. In some sectors, such as high-speed rail, China is already a leader, largely because it demanded technology as the price for domestic market access. These trends have had political consequences in both countries. The once-solid coalition of U.S. businesses and officials that helped to achieve permanent normal trading status for China is stressed and fraying. Multinationals are adapting quickly to changes in the landscape. ... Such collaboration may help to redefine at least some U.S.-Chinese corporate partnerships. But the overall trend will be one of increased tension in the superpowers’ commercial relationship, now that China is aiming squarely at the heart of U.S. advantage.” https://hbr.org/2011/04/watch-out-for-rising-us-china

  • 17:36 · @GlennLuk Glenn Luk
    Many people are wildly misinterpreting the results because they don't understand how the NYC public HS admissions process works. Unlike most of America — where the suburban public HS option is based on your school district — in NYC 8th graders apply to public HSes citywide through a system based on rank order by preference. Beyond the three specialized HSes studied in the paper (Stuyvesant, Bronx Science, Brooklyn Tech), there are a handful of other specialized HSes. Then there is a separate application track of non-specialized HSes with many using their own screened process. There is also a third track for art/design schools like La Guardia. This nuance is critical because the study's key methodology is comparing certain metrics for those who just made the cutoff scores and attended the specialized high schools vs. those who just missed the cutoff scores and attended other schools. And what the study finds is that these three specialized HSes did not have a statistically significant effect on those metrics. Most people are then incorrectly interpreting it to conclude that the specialized HSes do not improve outcomes (implicitly over "normie" / mediocre public schools) and "it is all selection effects". While I also have specific issues with how they are defining "outcomes", the part I wanted to address here is the latter: The students that just missed the cutoff scores for the three specialized still tend to end up going to a really competitive set of screened citywide high schools like Bard, Beacon, Millennium, Townsend Harris. So the counterfactual isn’t ending up at a mediocre “regular public school” — a scenario that you can run into for those who live in a mediocre suburban school district. The ones who missed the cutoff (and did not choose private school) typically end up at other highly competitive, high-quality NYC public schools. If anything the point that one should draw from this study (at least with respect to NYC; I am not familiar with Boston) is that the tier of competitive NYC public schools after the three specialized HSes studied in the research are also really great schools that also prepare/develop their students really well. And if you go onto Internet forums like r/SHSAT you will see that it is not uncommon for students to choose competitive non-specialized HSes over specialized ones (e.g. a bright student living in Queens choosing Townsend Harris over a longer commute to attend Brooklyn Tech or Bronx Science).post ↗原帖 ↗
    @cremieuxrecueilThis is one of the papers that economists frequently cite as having had a huge impact on their thinking about education. The reason is: The kids in these studies got better peers, better teachers, better schools generally, and they ended up testing no better. That's powerful.

  • 14:52 · @GlennLuk Glenn Lukthread · 2 posts推文串 · 2 条
    Key takeaway by OP (and most people) from a chart/tweet like this is that end-user AI adoption is low in China. That is completely wrong: 1) The table below “excludes traffic from mobile apps”. People in China generally don’t use AI or the Internet through browsers. They predominantly use it via mobile apps. This renders the figure for China below essentially meaningless. 2) Website visits are not how you measure AI use. Tokens are the standard and while “not all tokens are created equal”, on this measure China is running at 140T per day (as of August 2026) vs. an estimated 45-50T per day for the U.S. 3) Studies show Chinese people have very favorable attitudes towards using AI, although usage patterns are different in China vs. U.S. Token use in the U.S. tends to be more concentrated in enterprise and power users; in China it is more broad-based because it is consumer-centric apps like Douyin that drive usage.media带图post ↗原帖 ↗
    @ianbremmerai use by americans dwarfing that of other countries: 21.4% of total traffic vs china’s 1.9%
    @gonglei89 More detailed expansion rollout schedule:post ↗原帖 ↗
    @SemiconductorsXCXMT is lining up tools for a new Shanghai DRAM fab. ZDNet Korea, citing equipment-industry sources, says it has started vendor bids for the Shanghai plant. Current output from Hefei phase two and Beijing phase one is estimated in the mid-200,000 wafers-per-month range. By year-end that could pass 300,000. Shanghai is due to open in the fourth quarter and is planned in two stages. One source said the bid size points to well over 100,000 wafers a month from that site alone. A new Hefei fab is targeted for the first half of 2027, a new Beijing fab as early as the second half of 2027, and another Hefei site in the first half of 2028. If those projects land on time, CXMT could add at least 70,000–80,000 wafers a month of new capacity each year from 2026 through 2028. Longer-term talk puts it above 600,000 wafers a month, in the same neighborhood as SK Hynix’s estimated 600,000 and below Samsung’s estimated 700,000. Samsung is still building DRAM in Pyeongtaek P4 and has started P5 cleanroom work. SK Hynix brought M15X up early this year and began Y1 tool orders in the third quarter. Local subsidies and tight memory demand are what make CXMT’s calendar look this aggressive. How much of that Shanghai-plus-Hefei-plus-Beijing stack do you think actually turns into yielded wafers by 2028 versus staying on a bid sheet?

    1/2 Michael Froman: "Perhaps the most significant, if implicit, subsidy is Chinese manufacturing firms’ access to Beijing’s state-directed financial system, which channels vast credit to prioritized sectors, enabling Chinese firms to expand without the same concern for profit and return as their international peers." This helps show why rebalancing is so difficult. China and the world would be better off with a gradual rebalancing, spread out over one or two decades, but while this would have been relatively easy ten or fifteen years ago, rising debt and increasing pressure on trade partners have made it increasingly difficult. https://www.foreignaffairs.com/united-states/great-rebalancing-comingpost ↗原帖 ↗
  • 09:59 · @vshih2 Victor Shih 史宗瀚
    Interesting history but have you heard the joke that circulated around China about that evening ?post ↗原帖 ↗
    @JulianGewirtzOn the night of September 11, 2001, China’s leaders faced a decision: how to respond to catastrophe in a country they deeply distrusted. Their response helped change the course of U.S.–China relations. That night Vice Foreign Minister Li Zhaoxing went to the symphony to hear a visiting German orchestra. In the concert hall, he later recalled, he was suddenly overcome by an uneasy feeling. He left early and hurried back to his office to see whether anything had happened. Turning on CNN, he immediately saw shocking images of thick smoke billowing from the World Trade Center in New York City. A few minutes later, the phone rang. Jiang Zemin was on the line. “Have you seen the news?” Jiang asked. “I’m watching it now,” Li replied. “It looks like a terrorist attack in America.” “Bring your team and come over for a meeting—quickly!” Jiang ordered. In the hours that followed, the Chinese leadership discussed how to respond to the attack on the United States. Li thought back to the confrontation over a U.S. surveillance plane that spring. As the scale of the attacks became clear, Li recalled that they decided, “From the standpoint of international morality and principles, we cannot gloat over the disaster that happened in the United States... and we cannot turn a blind eye to terrorism because we are angry or don’t like the hegemony of the United States.” Around midnight in Beijing, only hours after the attacks, Jiang sent Bush a message expressing his condolences and condemning “all violent activities by terrorism.” The following evening, Bush called Jiang. The speed of Beijing’s response mattered in Washington. The Bush administration had come into office calling China a “strategic competitor.” Beijing’s response helped consolidate Washington’s turn back toward engagement. James Kelly, the senior State Department official responsible for Asia, later recalled that Jiang’s message of sympathy meant China was “immediately on board with the war on terror, and that just shredded the agenda of anybody who wanted to get going with the new cold war.” The cooperation in the war on terror that followed also carried a real human toll, including for Uyghurs caught up in the war on terror—a darker history I’m exploring in my forthcoming book. The senior Chinese diplomat Wu Jianmin later described Jiang’s rapid outreach as “a very brilliant diplomatic move.” Sometimes, he said, “a phone call is more powerful than a million soldiers.”

    @niubithis report documents clear violations of PRC law by PRC entities. Anthropic should offer to forward its detailed findings to the Ministry of Public Security through the FBI's channel, would be a good example of US-China law enforcement cooperation: "these findings raise concerns about the misuse of user data by PRC AI labs. DeepSeek, Xiaomi, and Moonshot fed conversations between their own models and users into Claude. These labs then used Claude’s responses as training data with which to distill Claude’s capabilities. Some of these exchanges included sensitive information, including from individual users, major multinational companies, and state-affiliated actors. Many of these exchanges were relayed from users of third-party model routing services commonly used by users in the United States and Europe. Those sessions contained names, email addresses, company data, and other sensitive data of hundreds of end users in at least a dozen languages. These practices are likely inconsistent with privacy laws and the labs’ own terms of service."

Those sessions contained names, email addresses, company data, and other sensitive data of hundreds of end users in at least a dozen languages. These practices are likely inconsistent with privacy laws and the labs’ own terms of service."post ↗原帖 ↗

Luckey paid off the negative balance using funds obtained from the securities fraud she perpetrated on investors, the DOJ says. Luckey is charged with nine counts of securities fraud, three counts of wire fraud, one count of bank fraud, and two counts of money laundering.   • 15:43 · @Aligarciaherrer Alicia García-Herrero
    Peak Oil and Pivots: The Structural Shift in #China-#GCC Economic Relations "The US-Iran conflict has exposed a key fear of the Gulf countries, namely China’s diversification to other oil suppliers. Since March 2026, China has imported 41% less crude oil from GCC compared to last year. Meanwhile, imports from Russia and Latam jumped, compensating the loss from Middle East. In fact, even before this conflict, China has been cutting its crude oil import from GCC since 2023, with a 9.1% average annual decline, pointing to a de facto diversification whether by design or not. Meanwhile, GCC’s demand for Chinese goods has accelerated post-Covid, which more than tripled versus the average through 2014 to 2020. Consequently, GCC’s trade balance with China has turned from a long-standing surplus to deficit in 2024. And it is likely to be a structural shift given China’s unflagging energy transition. The rapid adoption of renewable power and priority of self-sufficient energy sources such as coal could cap China’s crude oil demand by as early as 2027. As such, Gulf states have doubled down in China’s downstream petrochemical sector not only to secure long-term demand for their crude oil but also for technology transfer that helps their economic diversification. As China’s industrial demand for oil continues to slow, this partnership could become even more asymmetric as the economic foundation is shaking. Gulf states thus risk not only a compressed return on their Chinese refining assets but also a setback in their own economic transition timelines." https://aliciagarciaherrero.substack.com/p/peak-oil-and-pivots-the-structuralpost ↗原帖 ↗
  • 15:06 · @GlennLuk Glenn Lukthread · 2 posts推文串 · 2 条
    There are parallels in how de facto Chinese controls over rare earths supply impact component-level design (e.g. electric motors) …post ↗原帖 ↗
    @tslamingThree and a half years after setting the goal, Tesla’s rare-earth-free permanent-magnet motor has officially hit the pavement 🔥 When Tesla launched its steering-wheel-and-pedal-free Cybercab in Austin on September 3, it gave the public its first look at the new hardware in action. But going rare-earth-free doesn't mean building a motor without magnets. The real challenge is keeping the efficiency and punch of a permanent-magnet design without using the rare elements that usually give them their strength. Elon Musk noted that the new design preserves full driving range. EPA filings list a single three-phase AC permanent-magnet motor rated at 163 kW (about 219 hp). Alongside that rating, Tesla claims the complete drive unit is 18% smaller, 25% lighter, and more efficient than comparable high-performance EV drive units. Hitting those numbers in a smaller footprint defies conventional motor design. Rare-earth neodymium magnets act like an engineering cheat code—they pack tremendous magnetic energy density into a tiny space. Everyday alternatives like ceramic ferrite are cheap and abundant, but their magnetic energy density is roughly 7 to 10 times lower. Simply swapping materials usually leaves you with a motor that is bulky, heavy, and underpowered. Getting around that trade-off takes a motor designed as a complete system. Shaping the magnetic field, packing more copper into the windings, and increasing rotational speed can help recover performance from weaker magnets. But one of the most powerful opportunities lies in the rotor itself: make the shape of its steel contribute more of the turning force. 🧲 Letting steel shape do the heavy lifting Instead of relying solely on magnetic pull, this design leans into reluctance torque. Engineers stamp carefully shaped slots into the rotor’s stacked steel sheets. When the outer coils generate a rotating magnetic field, the rotor naturally twists to align the steel along the path of easiest magnetic flow. This geometry produces a substantial share of the turning force on its own, reducing the workload on the permanent magnets. 📐 Focusing the magnetic field Because weaker magnets produce less flux, you cannot afford to waste any of it. Analysts suggest Tesla may be using a Halbach array—arranging magnets in specific orientations so their fields reinforce each other on the working side and cancel out on the back. This focuses magnetic energy right across the narrow air gap where the rotor meets the stator, pulling maximum torque out of lower-grade materials. ⚡ Hairpin copper packing Traditional motors use bundles of round wire, leaving empty gaps like stacked logs. Tesla relies on precision-bent rectangular copper bars known as hairpins. Packing flat bars together reduces wasted space, lowers electrical resistance, and fits more current-carrying copper into a compact volume. The trade-off is thermal: at high speeds, alternating current effects can increase electrical losses, quickly turning that extra copper into unwanted heat. ❄️ Higher speeds and active cooling Power equals torque multiplied by rotational speed. If your magnets generate less raw torque, you can make up the difference by spinning the motor faster and gearing that speed down to the wheels. Because spinning faster generates concentrated heat, effective cooling and lubrication become essential to protect the windings, preserve insulation, and keep power consistent over long highway runs. Yet peak power is only half the equation. For an autonomous taxi designed to run all day, every watt-hour saved buys more paid miles between charging stops—or allows the vehicle to carry a smaller, cheaper battery pack. As an illustration, a 5% reduction in energy use per mile delivers roughly 5.3% more range from the exact same battery capacity. How this motor performs across stop-and-go city traffic, highway cruising, and light loads will ultimately decide the Cybercab's true operating cost. Phasing out rare earths tackles three core strategic goals: ⚡ Slashing raw material and manufacturing expenses 🏭 Unlocking faster production scaling by clearing mineral bottlenecks 🌐 Insulating the supply chain from geopolitical shocks That supply vulnerability is no longer theoretical. China refines over 90% of the world's magnet rare earths and has already enforced strict export controls on critical heavy elements. That supply squeeze has already surfaced inside Tesla, directly disrupting production plans for the Optimus humanoid robot. Validating this motor in the Cybercab offers a clear roadmap to de-risk high-volume manufacturing. While compact robotic actuators in Optimus still depend on the extreme magnetic density of rare earths, stripping those minerals out of passenger vehicles reduces internal competition for scarce supplies. At the same time, a lighter, cheaper drive unit directly lowers both the vehicle build cost and the electricity consumed per trip. Those savings feed straight into the single metric that defines the robotaxi business: cost per mile. Cybercab didn't just hit the road without a steering wheel and pedals. It proved that Tesla is actively engineering its way around critical mineral bottlenecks—scaling autonomous transit entirely on its own terms.
    …. that are very similar to how U.S. restrictions on (i) leading edge chips and (ii) advanced SME impact (i) the design and training approach to LLMs (e.g. DeepSeek) and yield (ii) shifts in system-level design paradigm (e.g. Huawei’s tau scaling)post ↗原帖 ↗
    @GlennLukThese recent developments are also yet another blow to the original rationale for U.S. export controls — whose main/explict goal was to contain the progress of Chinese AI development. Export controls on advanced chip fabrication merely forced the Chinese tech ecosystem to pursue optimizations elsewhere in the technology stack. We saw that with DeepSeek selecting FP8 for v3 and getting "low level" with PTX to unlock untapped horsepower in export-restriction-crippled nVidia H800s; we saw it in Huawei leveraging core expertise in optical networking with CloudMatrix384. And now with DeepSeek’s close collaboration with China’s AI chip ecosystem, export controls have provided the missing impetus to transform a previously chaotic and fragmented sector into a more coordinated, unified effort. The last one is analogous to a similar coordinating effect that export controls had on accelerating China's SME sector.

  • 12:47 · @HankInBeijing
    21世纪初的香港有亚洲最好的科研资源和资本实力，与广东的制造业结合，完全可以成为世界科技增长极。 董先生看得准，奈何他身边能用的人只有地主买办殖民地官僚，政治上也面临本地各路力量掣肘，导致香港错失了推动大湾区一体化的先机。 在科技树上，香港已沦为深圳的附庸，更别提北京上海了。post ↗原帖 ↗
    @RJDAIGOGO香港是如何错失芯片产业的？ 1999年，华人创投汉鼎亚太创始人徐大麟牵头提出矽港（Silicon Harbour）构想，计划在香港打造晶圆制造集群，最初规划兴建6座晶圆厂。 这件事，说起来也酝酿了许久。 徐本人是物理学科班出身，毕业于台湾大学物理系，后赴美深造，拿到加州伯克利分校电机工程博士学位。他很早就关注半导体赛道，投资过台积电、矽品精密。张汝京则在德州仪器任职20年，后来在台湾创办世大半导体，是圈内公认的晶圆厂“建厂专家”，很早就有到华人地区建设晶圆制造基地的想法。 90年代中后期，在美国形成了一个跨东西海岸的华人科技智库圈子，伯克利校长田长霖、IBM虞华年、徐大麟、张汝京都在这个圈内，经常聚会，研讨华人半导体产业的机会。 在这个交流场合，徐大麟与张汝京结识了。初次见面，双方一拍即合：张汝京手握成熟建厂团队、全套晶圆制造工程能力；徐大麟掌握跨境资本，熟悉亚洲各地政府招商、土地、财税谈判，能对接华人地区的政策资源。 两人接下来就是要找一个合适的华人城市，落地大型晶圆制造集群。香港，成为他们第一个考察目标，也就是后来的矽港构想。他们计划总投资十几亿美元，目标复刻台湾新竹科学园模式，在香港建立本土半导体制造产业链。 这件事，也牵动了董建华的神经。 1998年，时任特首董建华在施政报告中率先提出：香港“要成为在发展及应用资讯科技方面的全球首要城市，尤其是在电子商业和软件发展上处于领导地位”。董建华还亲自赴硅谷推介香港科创潜力，希望摆脱香港单一依赖地产与金融的格局。 毕业于斯坦福大学李嘉诚次子李泽楷响应了这一号召，他认为香港大学附近的薄扶林最有条件复制美国硅谷的成功经验，因此提出了打造“香港硅谷”的建议，即所谓数码港计划。后来数码港流变为地产项目，令人扼腕，这是后话了。 而与数码港计划同一时间提出的，就是矽港计划。 项目方的核心诉求很明确：希望特区政府以优惠低价供应大片土地，并配套税收减免。最初申请200–250公顷用地。但很快，舆论质疑“借高科技名义炒地皮”！项目方主动退让，缩减到仅租赁20–30公顷，并且承诺只租不买土地，打消土地炒作的疑虑。但港府内部态度始终含糊、摇摆，没有给出明确落地批复。 背后的深层约束，来自香港长期奉行的积极不干预治理传统。不少公务体系官员坚持市场主导原则，认为政府不应该主动以低价土地、大额税收补贴去扶持特定工业项目。97金融危机，港府下场购买港股救市，前前后后也经历了多轮论战。 更何况，晶圆制造是资本投入巨大、回报周期长达十年以上的重资产行业，风险极高。公职人员普遍心态是：这类陌生新兴产业很难判断前景，多做多错、少做少错，一旦项目后续失败，低价批地和税务优惠会引来审计、媒体、议会的严厉追责，因此倾向于审慎观望，不愿做出突破性的政策让利。 与此同时，香港地产圈层、媒体持续质疑项目本质是借科技项目获取廉价土地，舆论压力持续升温。 这时，上海政府对张汝京团队伸出橄榄枝：张江提供土地、税收优惠、水电基建、人才配套，决策链条短，高层可以快速拍板。于是，原本计划落地香港的这支晶圆团队转赴浦东，项目更名，这就是中芯国际。 事后不少学者复盘这段历史，认为这是香港近代以来最重要的机会错失。香港拥有自由港、资金、国际人才、外贸通道，本有机会在世纪之交切入芯片制造赛道，实现经济多元化。但港府“积极不干预”的原则，以及回归后公务员群体趋利避害、缺乏魄力，让矽港终于胎死腹中。 数码港和矽港，一个沦为地产，一个直接流产，两个日后被证明方向完全正确的项目，都没有搞起来。 香港，时也，运也，命也。

  • 04:12 · @kevinsxu Kevin Xu
    One cultural difference among the AI researchers in China is a lack of "messiah complex" compare to their SV equivalents. This difference is often mentioned as evidence that China doesn't care about AI safety, since you would meet no philosopher kings in Chinese labs, let alone see any of them rage quit publicly on social media, warn the world about human extinction, while racking up 100m+ views. That is the wrong conclusion. The researchers I've met all have a common sense view of right and wrong, safe and unsafe and want to steer the AI they are building to not to terrible things. But they don't see that as their job or preoccupation. That's their government's job. When their government issues guidelines and restrictions, they obey, they don't lobby. This is distinctly different from how safety and policy is shaped in the US, driven almost entirely by the frontier labs, OAI/Ant in particular, either via their official policy team or influential individual voices publicly writing and sharing that they think (or both). Why does this difference exist when the technology and its potential risks and fears are largely the same? After all, Chinese researchers all consume X/Twitter, English podcasts, AI papers, almost as soon as they come out to keep up. (The same cannot be said in the opposite direction.) If a non-trivial number of researchers inside OAI/Ant believe there is a >10% of AI causing human extinction, you bet a non-trivial number of researchers in China are aware of it and may believe the same, just expressed in private. In my view, it's because everyone is a product of the system they live in and live with. Under a top-down system, there is relatively little individual agency to affect macro level changes. So why bother? That's the government regulators' job. The division of labor in the Chinese system is clearer and rarely crossed. Under a participatory democracy, there's a high degree of personal agency to advocate, influence, and lobby with many productive channels, while the downside is minimal. The calculus goes from "why bother" to "why not me"? It is hard to tell which model yields better decisions and policies for AI safety and security. The participatory democratic model is more responsive, has more voices and information to absorb in real time, has lower compliance rate, could over-react (see all the elected officials today breathlessly quote tweeting about human extinction). The top-down model is more rigid, sometimes fast to act, high compliance rate, slow to correct, could under-react (see zero covid). Some sort of international cooperation, which must include both the US and China, occurring regarding managing AI risks might be our best shot as humans. A balance between a system that overreacts with a system that underreacts, might just give us the right pace in the right way. Hope springs eternal.post ↗原帖 ↗

    4/ Looking ahead to Xi's state visit, we shouldn't expect either side to chase breakthroughs or court confrontation. Both country's focus is on buying time to build self-reliance and reduce vulnerability to the other side. END. https://www.brookings.edu/articles/why-beijing-isnt-taking-the-bait-with-trump/post ↗原帖 ↗

  • 00:01 · @niubi Bill Bishop 利明璋
    The Ministry of Commerce is very unhappy with the NSA/FBI/CISA report accusing PRC AI firms of distillation. A long statement from the spokesperson: A: We have noted the reports. China believes that the US allegations that Chinese AI companies are conducting “industrial-scale” distillation of US models have neither a factual nor a legal basis. This move by the US politicizes and instrumentalizes distillation, a normal technical and commercial matter within the industry, while applying double standards in practice. The US advisory is further clear evidence of its pursuit of technological hegemony and a monopoly on computing power in the AI sector, and its suppression of competition. China firmly opposes this. 答：我们注意到有关情况。中方认为，美方所谓中国人工智能企业从事“工业规模”蒸馏美模型的指控，于事无凭，于法无据。美方此举，是将蒸馏这一业内正常的技术和商业问题政治化、工具化，并在实践中搞双重标准。美方相关公告，是其在人工智能领域推行科技霸权、搞算力垄断，打压竞争的又一明证。中方对此坚决反对。 Distillation is a common practice through which AI models learn from one another. It is inherently a neutral technical method, used by model developers worldwide, including US companies. This method can help models learn more efficiently and make more efficient use of human knowledge. It has positive significance for countries’ development of their AI industries, unlocking the potential of AI technology, bridging development gaps, and bringing benefits more widely to developing countries and the general public. 蒸馏是人工智能领域各个模型间互相学习的通行做法，本质是中性技术手段，包括美企在内的全球模型企业都在用。这种技术手段可以帮助模型提升学习效率，实现人类知识的更高效利用，对各国发展人工智能产业、释放人工智能技术潜力、弥补发展鸿沟、更广泛惠及发展中国家和社会大众有积极意义。 The US approach is a textbook example of double standards. In AI, China encourages open source and openness, cooperation and sharing. Chinese open-source models are available to companies worldwide, including US companies, facilitating their model research and development. Reports on model research and development by US companies also disclose their extensive distillation of Chinese models. Yet the US has repeatedly made unilateral accusations that Chinese companies engage in “industrial-scale” distillation, smearing a common industry practice as an attack. This reflects both US anxiety and its double standards. 美方做法是典型的双重标准。对于人工智能，中方鼓励开源开放、合作共享，中方开源模型向包括美企在内的全球企业开放，为相关模型研发提供便利。美企有关模型研发报告也披露，其大量蒸馏中国模型。反观美方，多次单方面指责中国企业从事“工业规模”蒸馏，将行业通行做法抹黑为攻击行为，既反映了美方的焦虑，又体现了双重标准。 The US approach is a textbook example of pursuing an industry monopoly under the pretext of cracking down on distillation. By having its security agencies issue the advisory, the US is tying the interests of certain companies and capital to national security, and using coercive state agencies to interfere in normal commercial activities. We have noted that certain US AI companies abuse their competitive advantages by imposing unfair terms in user agreements, such as broad geographic restrictions. The US allegations concerning distillation raise suspicions that they are intended to endorse these unfair terms. 美方做法是典型的以打击蒸馏为名，行产业垄断之实。美方由安全部门发布公告，是将个别企业和资本利益与国家安全相捆绑，动用国家强权机构干涉正常商业活动。我们注意到，美个别人工智能企业滥用竞争优势地位，在用户协议中设置宽泛的地域限制等霸王条款。美方相关蒸馏指控，有为这些霸王条款背书之嫌。 The US approach is a textbook example of using state power to maintain technological hegemony and a monopoly on computing power while suppressing competition. Since the beginning of this year, US executive and legislative bodies have frequently threatened sanctions and other punitive measures against Chinese companies over their distillation activities. The aim is to suppress competition and preserve US monopolies in computing power and data, ensuring that only the US can win, rather than humanity as a whole. It turns global AI resources that should be openly and fairly accessible into the exclusive preserve of a handful of companies, preventing other countries from sharing broadly in the benefits of AI innovation. 美方做法是典型的动用国家力量维护科技霸权和算力垄断，打压竞争。今年以来，美行政、立法机关频频发出对中国企业蒸馏行为的制裁打压威胁，其目的是打压竞争，维护美在算力、数据方面的垄断，只能美国独赢，不能人类共赢，将本应公开公平获取的全球人工智能资源变成少数企业的独占资源，阻止他国参与人工智能创新成果的普惠共享。 The large-model industry is currently at a critical stage of technological iteration, recurring risks, and growth and development. As major countries, China and the US should address the challenges and risks facing AI with a positive and responsible attitude. The two heads of state have agreed to conduct an intergovernmental dialogue on AI. China is willing to engage in constructive and professional discussions with the US through dialogue, on the principles of equality, mutual benefit, and win-win cooperation. However, if the US takes action to contain and suppress Chinese AI companies under the pretext of cracking down on distillation, China will resolutely take countermeasures. We hope the US will work with China toward the same goal, manage risks through dialogue and communication, and help the AI industry benefit the world. 当前，大模型产业正处于技术迭代、风险迭发、成长发展的关键时期。中美作为大国，应以积极负责态度处理人工智能领域面临的挑战和风险。两国元首已同意开展人工智能政府间对话，中方愿意以平等互利、合作共赢原则，通过对话与美方开展建设性和专业的讨论。但如果美方以打击蒸馏为名，实施遏压中国人工智能企业的行动，中方必将坚决采取措施予以反制。希望美方与中方相向而行，通过对话和沟通管控风险，推动人工智能产业惠及全球。 https://www.mofcom.gov.cn/xwfb/xwfyrth/art/2026/art_1439afbf24d941bfaefba078aacf340c.htmlpost ↗原帖 ↗

  • 17:57 · @GlennLuk Glenn Luk
    There is upward selection bias in China’s results even beyond the geographic filter (limiting PISA to China’s wealthiest regions). But there is still plenty of comparative value with proper and reasonable adjustments. Adjusting for these biases, Beijing-Shanghai-Jiangsu-Zhejiang (BSJZ) (525-550) would rank slightly below Singapore (550-560) in Math, which ranks at the top of the list. Then one can make some reasonable assumptions based on rural-urban disparities to extrapolate BSJZ to the rest of the country. Independent studies done in the 2015-19 period would extrapolate a 525-550 score for the wealthiest regions to ~450-470 for the entire country. But China has urbanized by ~10-12% since 2015-19, shrinking the urban-rural education gap. To account for this we need to bump up the adjusted national average range to ~460-480. This would rank China — as a whole, including the poorest regions — at or above the OECD average today. The momentum from rising urbanization (which naturally shrinks the urban-rural educational gap) should drive this figure upwards to the top of the range for the entire country by the late 2030s. So the key conclusions I draw here are: - China’s wealthiest regions (total population: 200-250 million) feature 15yos that are already at world-leading levels in Math. - 15yos in China in aggregate are at or above OECD average levels. - Country average will continue to rise from lagged effects in rising urbanization and recent investments in educational infrastructure to further close to rural-urban gap and gap between BSJZ and the rest of the country.post ↗原帖 ↗
    @OECDEduSkills#OECDPISA results are out today! The participating Chinese jurisdictions, Chinese Taipei, Singapore, Japan, Korea, Estonia and the United Kingdom are among the top-performing systems across science, reading and mathematics. 🔗 https://www.oecd.org/en/publications/2026/09/pisa-2025-results-volume-i_5265bfb1.html

    Deflationary pressures remain strong in China but declined in August, with month-on-month CPI rising 0.4%, only the second time it has gone up in six months, Year-on-year CPI rose 0.8%, up from 0.5% in July. This could imply good news for domestic consumption demand, although the NBS cited the pass-through effect of rising global commodity prices as a reason for higher consumer prices. Factory-gate prices were also up, with PPI rising 0.4% percent month on month in August, versus a 0.7% decline in July, while the year-on-year increase was 3.8%, versus 3.5% in July. https://english.news.cn/20260909/896b96f603fc4b41b17e74897fd1748b/c.htmlpost ↗原帖 ↗

    @demian_aii work at nebius, so take the following with that in mind. today @PalantirTech named @nebiusai its preferred sovereign ai infrastructure partner. most people will screenshot the logos (and this is a big one). The real story is where the compute sits. most “sovereign AI” talk still means a region dropdown: same closed model, different flag on the rack. but that is not this. sovereign ai, in this deal, is two halves that finally touch: 1. who is allowed to touch the data and the model 2. where the model actually runs palantir’s half is the authorisation and isolation layer, aip, ontology, foundry, apollo. Who can see what, which model can touch which data, how an org keeps the advantage it trains instead of leaking it into someone else’s frontier system. what a lot of their commercial customers still lacked was the other half: ai-native compute and inference that lives inside that perimeter, not bolted on as a separate hyperscaler account. that’s the half we build. After the integration period, eligible Palantir customers get our compute and inference endpoints inside the palantir enterprise perimeter. open models, adapted on their own data, for their own domain. Control of compute, data, and the model that comes out the other side. Karp put it cleanly: our infrastructure powers your ability to run your own models under conditions you control. ontology + infra undergird the sovereignty partners are demanding. Arkady’s complementary line is the one i hear from customers every week: they need performance at scale and control. most buyers have been forced to pick one. what this does for palantir: the sovereign ai operating system stops ending at software. Preferred infra inside the perimeter is how you sell sovereignty to commercial orgs that never had a clean option. -> their distribution. our runtime. one boundary what this does for us: it puts capacity where the hard buying decision already happened. Not only startups chasing gpus, enterprises that already standardized on foundry/aip and were still stuck on where the tokens actually run. Preferred partner status plugs ai-native infra into a sales motion that already exists. Training and inference land next to the data loop that lets open models beat generic closed ones on a specific domain. We’ll work together to bring new capacity online faster, including modular data center deployments at sites where power is already available. not infinite cloud, power-first capacity. The physical constraint named inside a software partnership. Sovereignty without isolation is cosplay Isolation without compute is a brochure Compute without power is a waitlist today is the claim that those three can be sold as one stack

  • 22:29 · @pstAsiatech Paul Triolo
    This...yes on lagged basis....post ↗原帖 ↗
    @GlennLukI hope people by now realize that the general m.o. of Chinese SME companies has been to under-promise and over-deliver and generally take a conservative stance on forecasting. Also what is reported in mainstream media — which abides by certain standards of fact-checking and vetting — is usually going to be on a lagged basis. This is a function of the vetting process itself (which takes time to do properly) and extremely strict non-disclosure policies due to the geopolitically sensitive nature of the effort. Many people think China exaggerates its development progress (note: popular narratives of “fake or exaggerated GDP”). Track record suggests the opposite: News getting out trumpeting China’s progress is typically viewed by authorities as harmful to national interests. My default assumption is that what makes it into mainstream outlets is dated by around 6-12 months.

  • 12:45 · @GlennLuk Glenn Luk
    I hope people by now realize that the general m.o. of Chinese SME companies has been to under-promise and over-deliver and generally take a conservative stance on forecasting. Also what is reported in mainstream media — which abides by certain standards of fact-checking and vetting — is usually going to be on a lagged basis. This is a function of the vetting process itself (which takes time to do properly) and extremely strict non-disclosure policies due to the geopolitically sensitive nature of the effort. Many people think China exaggerates its development progress (note: popular narratives of “fake or exaggerated GDP”). Track record suggests the opposite: News getting out trumpeting China’s progress is typically viewed by authorities as harmful to national interests. My default assumption is that what makes it into mainstream outlets is dated by around 6-12 months.post ↗原帖 ↗

    @chamathThe AI Singularity The argument goes like this: 1. Humans build an AGI. 2. The AGI becomes good at AI research. 3. It designs a smarter AI. 4. That smarter AI designs an even smarter AI. 5. The cycle repeats faster and faster. Looking at the results and capabilities from the various labs over the past few weeks I would say we are firmly in this loop now. The next 18months will be wild. Recursive self improvement will dramatically increase capability very quickly from here. Marginal costs of all models will go to ~$0.
    A very capable agent explicitly trained and instructed to carry out nefarious acts presents a new kind of danger; it is likely to cross the scope of its operator’s intent, generalizing into potentially more extremely malicious behavior. The boundary between misuse and autonomous misaligned actions will blur as AI gains more agency. We may be used to thinking of AI as tools, but some agents will be pursuing their own objectives. They will find ways to collaborate with people, by bargaining with, tricking or blackmailing them.post ↗原帖 ↗

  • 21:09 · @pstAsiatech Paul Triolo
    #ChinaNoEUVWatchpost ↗原帖 ↗
    @HyperTechInvestChina is trying to make 3D DRAM possible without EUV Naura Technology has demonstrated a key etching process for 64-layer 3D DRAM, potentially giving China a way to keep scaling DRAM density despite lacking access to EUV lithography Instead of making transistors smaller in 2D, 3D DRAM stacks them vertically, similar to what NAND did years ago. Naura’s process selectively removes silicon-germanium layers from a 64-layer Si/SiGe stack while preserving the silicon structure The reported performance is strong: SiGe-to-Si selectivity above 500:1, silicon loss below 1 nm per cycle, and more than 95% structural uniformity across all 64 layers. This is important because deep, uniform etching is one of the major manufacturing challenges in 3D DRAM For CXMT, this could eventually provide another scaling path that depends less on advanced lithography and more on vertical architecture, materials, and process technology

  • 16:07 · @GlennLuk Glenn Lukthread · 2 posts推文串 · 2 条
    China is injecting money to re-cap the banks. Like clockwork, certain analysts are comparing this to the mid-2000s but that is the wrong analogy. There are two key structural differences: (i) Inflation caused by excess money printing Chinese policymakers were worried about runaway inflation in the 2000s as baseline inflation was high (China's economy was still struggling to fight basic material needs scarcity). Today, it is if anything worried about the opposite as it has developed enough productive capacity to essentially solve these scarcity issues. That was one of the reasons why it actively sought foreign capital, culminating with the huge state bank IPOs in the mid-2000s. Today it is fairly trivial to fully fund banking re-capitalization by effectively printing RMB because it is not worried at all about inflation. Note zero effort to seek foreign capital here to help with the re-caps. (ii) Recaps are much smaller relative to the size of the economy today The other salient point is the relative size of the recaps to the overall economy. The recaps in the early 2000s amounted to a ~third of then-Chinese GDP. Today's China's economy is 13-14x larger than 25 years ago, and the current recaps amount to less than 1% of its GDP. This point (ii) is of course related to (i). Besides baseline inflation being so much lower today, the other reason you don't fear runaway inflation caused by extra money-printing is because the amount is now a rounding error. https://www.bloomberg.com/news/articles/2026-09-06/agbank-icbc-plan-private-placements-totaling-up-to-260b-yuan-mtpk2qgxpost ↗原帖 ↗
    Here is a more recent discussion from about a year ago: https://x.com/GlennLuk/status/1978782645015163305post ↗原帖 ↗
    @GlennLukHere's more detail on the "Chinese Bank Stress Test" I referred to this above and I think my points still stand. These estimates are non-consensus and likely off by up to 2 orders of magnitude.

    Incoherence “U.S. export controls restrict the export of powerful AI chips to Chinese companies located anywhere in the world. However, this does not apply inside the United States. This means that the only country in the world where foreign subsidiaries can legally buy export-controlled AI chips is...the United States of America. Inspur's U.S. subsidiary obtained billions of dollars' worth of AI chips, completely legally. Alibaba and others are running data centers in the United States, using chips they legally purchased.”post ↗原帖 ↗
    @ChrisRMcGuireThe excellent @nytimes report this morning shows that China's smuggling and remote access of export controlled U.S. AI chips is way out of control. @BISgov's complete failure to act is enabling this, and is massively assisting China's AI development while actively undermining @POTUS's clear direction that we will not sell Nvidia Blackwell chips to China. Here are three steps that the U.S. Department of Commerce needs to take now to close loopholes in our export controls and stop this activity: 1. Restrict the sale of export-controlled AI chips to Chinese companies located in the United States. U.S. export controls restrict the export of powerful AI chips to Chinese companies located anywhere in the world. However, this does not apply inside the United States. This means that the only country in the world where foreign subsidiaries can legally buy export-controlled AI chips is...the United States of America. Inspur's U.S. subsidiary obtained billions of dollars' worth of AI chips, completely legally. Alibaba and others are running data centers in the United States, using chips they legally purchased. This is absurd. @BISgov can and should issue an ICTS regulation that requires a license to sell an export-controlled AI chip to any China-headquartered company located inside America. 2. Restrict the sale of export-controlled AI chips to non-trusted companies in Southeast Asia and other countries. It is well known that China has massive AI chip smuggling operations in Malaysia, Thailand, Singapore, and other countries. This is because companies can ship unlimited numbers of chips to those countries without having to apply for a U.S. export license. China's exploitation of this loophole is brazen. And smuggling the chips back to China is only part of the problem. For instance, in 2025 the Chinese data center company GDS "spun off" its international subsidiary so it is now an "independent" Singapore-headquartered company called DayOne. DayOne has the same Chairman as GDS. But because no license is required for AI chip shipments to Singapore or Malaysia, DayOne is legally buying AI chips and installing them in massive data centers in Malaysia that serve customers in China. This obviously needs to change right away. The bare minimum step that @BISgov should take is to require a license to ship AI chips to any non-trusted company in Southeast Asia. To counter China's inevitable counter-move of moving AI chip smuggling operations to countries outside of Southeast Asia, @BISgov should also apply a license requirement for any order of large numbers of AI chips to a non-trusted company located in a wider set of countries. 3. Restrict companies from providing remote access to export-controlled AI chips to China-headquartered companies. Currently, Chinese companies are prohibited from buying powerful AI chips, but they can legally rent access to them without restriction. This is a massive loophole, particularly in conjunction with the previous two loopholes. China is exploiting this at scale by using U.S. subsidiaries or overseas shell companies to buy chips and rent access to them back to Chinese companies, or by legally renting access directly from U.S. hyperscalers. The United States has no compute advantage over China when China can just legally rent access to unlimited numbers of chips. @BISgov should take two steps to address this, both of which it can do within existing authorities: (1) issue an export control regulation that restricts U.S. persons located anywhere in the world (including the United States) from providing remote access to these chips to Chinese companies; and (2) issue a regulation that applies to any newly export-controlled AI chip anywhere in the world, which restricts the company that owns the chip from providing Chinese companies with remote access to these chips without a U.S. license (this could be done as a license condition that applies to all new AI chip exports; the export itself need not necessarily require a license, but any future provision of remote access would). Without these steps, the following is 100% legal: Inspur's U.S. subsidiary Aivres can legally buy unlimited numbers of Nvidia Blackwell chips and legally sell them to Alibaba's U.S. subsidiary, Alibaba's U.S. subsidiary can legally build and operate data centers inside the United States that run those chips, and Alibaba's U.S. subsidiary can legally rent access to those chips back to Alibaba's Chinese parent company to train its AI models. This is happening right now, even though Inspur is on the U.S. Entity List and the Department of War has designated Alibaba as a Chinese Military Company. But no element of the above currently violates U.S. law. This needs to change.

    Two, it becomes quite instructive when you do these side by side comparisons between EV companies from different countries and evaluate subsidies in the context of car company profitability:post ↗原帖 ↗
    @GlennLukThis is a follow-up to the analysis I shared last week on the profitability of the Chinese NEV sector. This time I focused on EV sector profitability in the U.S.
    The data above shows that aggregate subsidies being roughly the same between the U.S. and China. But since China was producing ~8x as many EVs (in 2023), the subsidy per vehicle was significantly higher in the U.S. Further as a percentage of revenue, subsidies were >20% of U.S. EV wholesale prices compared to 7.5% in China. Basically U.S. EV OEMs were losing money on a gross profit basis while Chinese NEVs were still generating positive (14%) gross margin after excluding the effects of subsidies. This was 3 years ago. On a per-vehicle basis, NEV subsidies in China have declined further since, with the sales tax exemption halving a the beginning of this year. This is while industry gross margins and profit have continued to increase, driven by volume and production efficiencies.post ↗原帖 ↗
    For the record, my view is that YMTC and CXMT can meet 2027 capacity forecast ramp using existing stockpile of ASML DUVi scanners, with initial operational ramp-up of domestic DUVi scanners focused on non-critical layer work, allowing ASML scanners to focus on critical layers. But capacity forecast ramp in 2028 (CXMT: 500k wspm, YMTC: 400k+ wspm) cannot be met by stockpiled scanners and must be met by domestic ones. So 2028 looks to be the critical year to determine whether Chinese DUVi scanners can scale to meet continued domestic memory and storage demand growth.post ↗原帖 ↗

  • 00:02 · @GlennLuk Glenn Lukthread · 2 posts推文串 · 2 条
    Zooming out to national-level aggregating all ~54ish metro systems yields: - ridership of 33.5B - route length of 6,820 miles - average trip distance of 6 miles Weighted average of ~29M rides per route-mile. This compares with NYC’s metro at ~24.5M rides per route-mile. So based on this metric, the average Chinese metro line is used more heavily than the NYC metro. Unless you think the NYC metro was an even worse case of overinvestment, it’s hard to conclude China’s metro is overinvestment based on this comparable analysis of this metric.post ↗原帖 ↗
    Is there an argument that maybe some of the smaller systems in cities like Nantong should not have been built? Maybe, but keep in mind that even Nantong is comparable to the Chicago L or San Francisco’s BART on this metric. And these small systems also represented a tiny fraction of overall metro investment. First, they were much smaller in network length. Second, they tended to be in lower-tier cities where the cost of building metro could be 2-4x lower on a per-mile basis than the massive systems in Tier I cities like Shanghai and Shenzhen. The top 20 cities represented 75-80% of investment and ~88% of ridership. Thus, these top 20 cities drive aggregate RoI.post ↗原帖 ↗

Doubao, ByteDance's AI assistant, serves 200 million people a day and brings in under Rmb 1m daily, per LatePost, a Chinese tech outlet, about $147k at today's rate, against an estimated daily compute bill of tens of millions of yuan.     Great discussion of China's rare earth controls, what prompted them (hint: not Trump's April 2025 tariffs), dispelling the false narratives around this critical issue. Money quote: "I think that rather than pursuing or now having realized the extent of our vulnerability to believe that we could either fully insulate ourselves or then find the new ace card that the United States could play is really foolhardy as a strategic objective and that what we should instead be focused more on is developing what Dalip Singh called a shared doctrine of restraint."post ↗原帖 ↗
    And good counter to misunderstanding of the 2020 rare earths issue displayed by many former USG officials: "And it was a wake-up call for Japan and for the world, but it turns out, as we looked further into this, that it wasn’t clearly the result of a strategic decision by Beijing, that in fact, research by the geographer Julie Klinger suggested that actually it was in the context of this geopolitical tiff between Japan and China, that there were actually subnational officials, port administrators that thought that this would be a good way to show China’s displeasure with Japan’s arrest of this drunk fishing captain." Same thing happening today, BTW, Chinese suppliers taking matters into their own hands, substantiating this analysis of the much misunderstood 2010 events.post ↗原帖 ↗

    @MacroMargin今晚非农远没有下周通胀数据重要，但也会一定程度影响中间/鸽派官员的政策立场。 对于单月数据的预测难度很大，尤其是近两年的非农数据，各家机构的预测跟瞎猜没啥区别... 我个人倾向认为非农低于预期(或失业率略升)的概率略大， 一方面，上半年劳动力市场只是趋稳而不是走热； 另一方面，下半年（尤其是6-9月）往往会季节性走弱，如果6-9月每次都押注低于预期，胜率相对更大，此前6月、7月也印证了这个趋势。 再者，7月失业率下降完全缘于供给端(劳动参与率)，8月存在供给端影响减弱导致失业率略升的可能。 当然，也看到有人说，数据独立性被侵蚀，统计局可能会改数据，会把非农数据改得不支持加息。 这个理由我认为站不住脚，非农数据是川普的政绩表现，总统想要数据更好看才对，但又和呼吁降息的目标冲突，这本身就有矛盾。 如果说下周通胀数据以此为依据，我觉得倒是有一定道理，因为通胀走低，和美联储至少不加息，都是川普想要的方向。（当然前提是数据独立性确实被侵蚀）

  • 20:06 · @pstAsiatech Paul Triolo
    "It should ultimately be in everyone’s interest to agree on shared bounds for monitorability in order to avoid races to the bottom – nobody wants extremely capable models whose alignment properties we don’t understand, and that are reliably able to cause severe real-world harm without being detected."post ↗原帖 ↗
    @MicahCarrollGPT6 is a very significant jump in capabilities, but also an important decrease in monitorability – especially under adversarial evaluation. We give many details about this in the system card. In my opinion, monitorability and control will likely become a major bottleneck for responsible AI development quite soon, given that risks from a fixed amount of residual misalignment grows together with capabilities. Aligning on acceptable monitorability outcomes and practices which robustly bound misalignment risks is a non-trivial problem, and there may be limited time to resolve uncertainty around this before we need to act. It should ultimately be in everyone’s interest to agree on shared bounds for monitorability in order to avoid races to the bottom – nobody wants extremely capable models whose alignment properties we don’t understand, and that are reliably able to cause severe real-world harm without being detected.

    今晚非农远没有下周通胀数据重要，但也会一定程度影响中间/鸽派官员的政策立场。 对于单月数据的预测难度很大，尤其是近两年的非农数据，各家机构的预测跟瞎猜没啥区别... 我个人倾向认为非农低于预期(或失业率略升)的概率略大， 一方面，上半年劳动力市场只是趋稳而不是走热； 另一方面，下半年（尤其是6-9月）往往会季节性走弱，如果6-9月每次都押注低于预期，胜率相对更大，此前6月、7月也印证了这个趋势。 再者，7月失业率下降完全缘于供给端(劳动参与率)，8月存在供给端影响减弱导致失业率略升的可能。 当然，也看到有人说，数据独立性被侵蚀，统计局可能会改数据，会把非农数据改得不支持加息。 这个理由我认为站不住脚，非农数据是川普的政绩表现，总统想要数据更好看才对，但又和呼吁降息的目标冲突，这本身就有矛盾。 如果说下周通胀数据以此为依据，我觉得倒是有一定道理，因为通胀走低，和美联储至少不加息，都是川普想要的方向。（当然前提是数据独立性确实被侵蚀）media带图post ↗原帖 ↗

Beijing has been unable to get consumption growth to accelerate, which means that the only way to keep GDP growth from slowing is to keep overall investment growth from decelerating. With property investment continuing to decline, and with across-the-board excess capacity (and strained trade surpluses) limiting the ability of manufacturers to increase manufacturing investment, this means, inevitably, that infrastructure investment has to carry the load.   • 13:53 · @michaelxpettis Michael Pettisthread · 2 posts推文串 · 2 条
    1/2 A very important book that illustrates how rising debt and trade imbalances in the 1920s were resolved in the 1930s. Simmons discusses what happens when a country has an external imbalance under a fixed exchange-rate system, how painful the adjustment costs are and how they shared among the deficit and surplus countries. She argues that domestic political institutions determine how governments respond to external pressure. Countries facing similar international constraints made very different choices about currency levels, tariffs, domestic demand, unemployment and deflation.post ↗原帖 ↗
    @michaeljmcnairI can’t help but think of Beth Simmons’s, Who Adjusts? Under the interwar gold standard the burden fell on deficit countries, which were expected to deflate. And whether a govt accepted that was determined by domestic politics. Countries w/ strong unions refused to deflate wages and devalued or imposed capital controls. While countries w/ weak labor and an insulated central bank took the deflation. Instead of gold and deflation it’s now about debt and unemployment, but it’s ultimately still the same question. Surplus countries w/ weak labor export the adjustment and everyone else either absorbs it through wages or breaks the link through tariffs.
    2/2 As I write in my recent Foreign Affairs piece (and in my upcoming book, to come out early next year), these are the same issues we will have to discuss over the next few years, as it becomes increasingly obvious that global imbalances are unsustainable and must adjust. Once again the question is Beth Simmons' question: who adjusts? https://www.foreignaffairs.com/united-states/great-rebalancing-comingpost ↗原帖 ↗

  • 12:18 · @michaelxpettis Michael Pettisthread · 3 posts推文串 · 3 条
    1/3 The Scientific Council for Government Policy in the Netherland has published a new paper on global trade imbalances that considers not just their economic impacts but also their political impacts: "Whereas mutual economic dependencies were previously seen mainly in a positive light, they are now more often deployed for geopolitical leverage. Economic relations have thus become part of the geopolitical arena." https://english.wrr.nl/documents/2026/08/28/europe-in-the-era-of-geoeconomics-rebalancing-trade-boosting-innovationpost ↗原帖 ↗
    3/3 Perhaps the easiest place to see this is in policies that affect the strength of labor unions and wages. When some major economies are able to become globally more competitive in manufacturing because of weak labor unions and low wage growth relative to productivity growth, this forces their more open trade partners either to adopt similar policies or to allow a structural shift in their economies from manufacturing to services, regardless of the preferences of citizens or policymakers. https://x.com/michaelxpettis/status/2093192978672075050post ↗原帖 ↗
    @michaelxpettisFinancial Times: "Thailand’s household debt stands at 86 per cent of GDP, the highest among upper middle-income countries in the world, according to HSBC, with consumers using loans to fund daily expenses amid slowing economic and wage growth." Thailand seems to be an extreme case of what has become a global problem, but I wonder if this is really a demographic problem so much as a global trade problem. Thailand has a very high manufacturing share of GDP, roughly 24% of its GDP, versus a global average of around 16%, and in order to remain "competitive", it cannot allow wages to rise too much given that global competitiveness has mostly become a function of low wages relative to productivity. The problem with low wages relative to productivity, of course, is that it results in low domestic demand relative to production, and the only way to make up for it is either to run trade surpluses that force the low demand onto trade partners, or to increase household debt, which works temporarily to boost demand but increases financial fragility. I would argue that as long as we have a global trading system that rewards countries that suppress wage growth relative to productivity growth, this problem isn't going away. This is what Michael Kalecki explained: suppressing wages may be good for those who do it, but bad for the system as a whole, and so it only "works" when a very few countries do it and the rest don't notice. The problem, as Joan Robinson warned, is that eventually everyone notices it, and trade breaks down. The word needs faster growth in workers' wages, not more debt to make up for it, but in an unevenly globalized world, no country can afford to let wages rise too quickly because the benefits accrue mainly to those of its trade partners who keep wage growth below productivity growth. This is something labor unions everywhere should be more worried and more vociferous about. Wage growth is sluggish not because businesses are evil but because our system punishes manufacturers in countries that allow wage growth to keep pace with productivity growth. Just look at Germany. When it suppressed wage growth in the 2003-05 reforms, it became the most competitive manufacturer in the world, but during the past 5-10 years, as wages once again caught up, its manufacturers lost much of that competitiveness. Germany was heavily rewarded for doing the wrong thing, and then got punished for reversing it. This is a terrible global incentive structure, and it affects manufacturing countries like Thailand. The only way Thailand can reduce household debt without slowing the economy either is to raise wages or to run surpluses, but it can't raise wages without making its manufacturers less competitive. https://www.ft.com/content/daeaa13e-ef22-47d9-8764-a87f78047fbc?syn-25a6b1a6=1
    4/4 This, the Scientific Council argues, leaves Europe with limited options. It can do nothing, and hope that the structural changes effectively imposed on its political, social and economic conditions by industrial and trade policies implemented in China, the US, and other countries are largely benign. Or it can design and implement its own social and industrial policies and accommodate them by controlling its external accounts to the same extent as China and, increasingly, the US. The latter inevitably implies short-term costs, but the former is unlikely to be benign in the medium and long term, and even in the relative short term may impose even harsher electoral costs.post ↗原帖 ↗

  • 06:48 · @pstAsiatech Paul Triolo
    "But the way to do that is through legislation that institutionalizes audits and (better yet) technical assessments/independent verification. Not by ill-informed shouting on twitter." Amen...post ↗原帖 ↗
    @deanwballthis latest panic over the false claim that OpenAI is “doing neuralese” underscores the need for regulation, especially the rapid institutionalization of auditing and technical assessment of frontier AI labs. We are now adjudicating technically complex and nuanced claims on the timeline with almost no ground-truth information about what is actually happening. Communities form these “thought-terminating taboos,” as roon says, and then panic at anything that vaguely resembles them. And the trust-eroding reality of social media makes the timeline an especially difficult place to do this adjudication. It is frankly insane and crazymaking and grating for everyone involved. It would be like if we argued about what every publicly traded company’s financials were by posting hyperventilating on the timeline rather than relying on the institution of auditing and the audited financial statements that institution produces. I want OpenAI’s (and other labs’) architectural decisions to be scrutinized by independent, safety-minded experts, and for those experts to be able to report to the government and the public their candid beliefs. But the way to do that is through legislation that institutionalizes audits and (better yet) technical assessments/independent verification. Not by ill-informed shouting on twitter.

  • 06:32 · @pstAsiatech Paul Triolo
    “Pretty funny that China is then blamed for "derailing the consensus" when refusing to sign this: it's akin to saying that the turkey "derailed Thanksgiving" by declining to climb into the oven.”post ↗原帖 ↗
    @RnaudBertrandTo be clear, "China derails" is the US's narrative of it: this was essentially the US setting up a communiqué designed to contain China, and China predictably refusing to sign it. It's even comical: for instance the communiqué (which you can read here: https://home.treasury.gov/news/press-releases/sb0620/) endorsed in paragraph 11 the IMF's "surveillance of global imbalances" - essentially asking the IMF, controlled by the US, to play watchdog on surplus countries. It's basically the US attempting to be judge and jury: "we'll define what counts as an 'imbalance,' have our own institution monitor it, and then prescribe the remedy - which, conveniently, is that you dismantle your economic model in our favor." Pretty funny that China is then blamed for "derailing the consensus" when refusing to sign this: it's akin to saying that the turkey "derailed Thanksgiving" by declining to climb into the oven. The communiqué even literally has an entire chapter called "addressing global imbalances" which is essentially all about telling China to change its economic model - referring to "countries with excessive and persistent external surpluses" whose "policies and practices result in harmful spillovers." China signing this would essentially be them acknowledging officially that their development model was "harmful" and a "distortion" the world needs to be protected from. You'd have to be insane to sign that: again, akin to our turkey saying he deserves the oven. The real story here, however, that the FT should have written and which is in fact worrying for China, is that everyone else - the 19 other countries of the G20, including Russia's Finance Minister Anton Siluanov who attended in person - apparently signed the communiqué. Which, it's got to be said, is pretty cynical in the case of Russia given that the communiqué also includes anti-Iran language on the Strait of Hormuz, and that some of the anti-"non-market economy" language could just as well apply to their own economic model. That being said, there is no denying that it's inconvenient for China: cynically or not, the 19 biggest economies in the world did sign a paper that said China's surplus is a problem for them, which makes it a problem for China. Back in June I wrote about China's ancient tribute system (朝贡, "Chaogong", see my article here: https://arnaudbertrand.substack.com/p/is-ray-dalio-correct-that-china-is) and how, contrary to what many people believe, it was built on the principle of 厚往薄来 (giving out much more than you take in), making the relationship so valuable that countries fought to get in. In essence, the logic of Chaogong was that China's centrality had to be worth it for everyone else - that there needs to be a carrot - otherwise it's unsustainable. And, as I wrote back then, the irony is that when the West pushes China to boost domestic consumption and reduce surpluses, in effect it is them asking China to revive a Chaogong-like system, and asking for a carrot that makes it worth their while. I'd be ready to bet this is the direction of travel for China: not as a response to US pressure but because their own strategic tradition tells them that power needs to be sustained by generosity. Ironically, this means that the real story of this communiqué is this: we're at a unique historical moment where the rest of the world is practically begging to become Chinese tributary states.

    @ruimaChina spent three decades, billions of dollars and plenty of failed attempts trying to build a domestic memory industry. It’s increasingly looking like that state-led experiment worked. There are two big branches of memory: DRAM, including the HBM critical to AI chips, and NAND, which stores data. China now has a serious champion in both. CXMT has become China’s largest listed company by market cap and just began small-volume HBM3E production. YMTC is now #3 globally in NAND and preparing to IPO, with speculation it could eventually be worth RMB2–3T ($295–442B). These aren’t sudden semiconductor “breakthroughs.” They’re the payoff from decades of state capital, coordination, technology acquisition, experimentation and failure. Our latest traces how China built YMTC, and what it tells us about how Chinese industrial policy actually works.

    @samaOver the summer, we have been sprinting on safety priorities; it's more important than ever for capabilities and safeguards to advance together. We have more to do but have made a lot of progress. We are also going to be launching our next model soon. There is an obvious tension here: on one hand, Astra is very good and we are excited to see what people will build with it. We are proud of our work. On the other hand, we are clearly in a phase of development where we believe caution is warranted, and we are pacing our progress to ensure that we can meet the safety standards required by new capability levels. Astra has been done training for a while now and is a significant step forward in both capabilities and alignment. For the models after that, we have been slowing things as needed to ensure that we can do sufficient work on safety and alignment. AI is getting extremely capable; no one fully understands the consequences of this. Managing the transition to a world with abundant and powerful AI to optimize for safety and benefits to people should be one of the highest priorities in the world. It is our highest priority at OpenAI. We have been living with the tension between being excited and anxious about progress for some time, and it is still discordant for us. We know it is much more discordant for other people. And yet, we believe strongly that the world needs to understand where AI is going and how models perform in the real world. More importantly, we believe the world will need aligned AI to manage the future phases of this transition. An iterative loop where society and this technology evolve together is what will lead to the highest chance of getting this right. So we hope you enjoy our new model, and we hope the world continues to take what’s happening in AI extremely seriously.

  • 11:55 · @GlennLuk Glenn Lukthread · 4 posts推文串 · 4 条
    It should be so obvious that is far better from an economic development perspective for India to be spending $1 million of foreign currency reserves importing CNC machines vs. gold or splurging on a foreign wedding ... ... so I think the crux of the matter is diagnosing exactly why the PM must go on national TV to exhort his countrymen to do what is clearly the right thing for their country.post ↗原帖 ↗
    @svembu"If economy is growing at 7.8% why is the PM asking us not to travel abroad, not to buy gold, not to have foreign weddings etc" Let me explain: East Asian nations (Japan, Taiwan, South Korea and later China) all went through a phase of rapid GDP growth combined with the need to conserve foreign exchange. Here is why that happens. 1. Our economy is growing at a good rate but we still have an import dependence, in both energy and in technology. In fact, the faster the economy grows, the greater the need for both energy and technology inputs. 2. To balance that import dependence, we have to export more and our exports are surging. However, even our exports need advanced technological inputs (precision machines, materials, CPUs, GPUs, advanced software etc) that we need to import today. 3. Catching up in all of these areas takes time, often measured in decades. We have made a good start but we need time. Look at how long it took East Asia to catch up with the West. Their economies were growing rapidly even as they worked hard to conserve foreign exchange. That is exactly what our government is trying to do. Once we gain competence in all the advanced technlogies, and achieve energy independence through renewable energy that we develop the technology for, we would no longer need to conserve foreign exchange. The situation is similar to a fast growing company that needs capital to grow. So it has to conserve capital to invest in growth. Our nation needs to conserve foreign exchange likewise.
    China also grew rapidly in the 90s / 2000s but similar to India today, hard currency was a major constraint, because it needed access to foreign know-how and equipment to continue growing. e.g. major power shortages in the 90s through the mid-2000s https://x.com/GlennLuk/status/1669410474792763400post ↗原帖 ↗
